  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addressing the fundamental challenge of understanding the world from distributed, partial viewpoints in embodied multi-agent systems, where each agent's ego-centric view is limited by occlusion and ambiguity.

Method: Proposes CoRL: a two-stage framework combining Chain-of-Thought supervised fine-tuning with reinforcement learning using Group-Relative Policy Optimization, featuring Cross-View Spatial Reward (CVSR) that provides dense task-aligned feedback linking reasoning steps to visual evidence.

Result: CoRL consistently surpasses strong proprietary and open-source baselines on the E2W benchmark across reasoning and perception-grounding metrics, generalizes to external spatial reasoning benchmarks, and enables effective real-world multi-robot manipulation with calibrated multi-camera rigs.

Conclusion: E2W and CoRL provide a principled foundation for learning world-centric scene understanding from distributed, ego-centric observations, advancing collaborative embodied AI through effective cross-view localization and grasp-and-place execution.

Abstract: Understanding the world from distributed, partial viewpoints is a fundamental challenge for embodied multi-agent systems. Each agent perceives the environment through an ego-centric view that is often limited by occlusion and ambiguity. To study this problem, we introduce the Ego-to-World (E2W) benchmark, which evaluates a vision-language model's ability to fuse heterogeneous viewpoints across three tasks: (i) global counting, (ii) relational location reasoning, and (iii) action-oriented grasping that requires predicting view-specific image coordinates. To address this setting, we propose CoRL, a two-stage framework that combines Chain-of-Thought supervised fine-tuning with reinforcement learning using Group-Relative Policy Optimization. Its core component, the Cross-View Spatial Reward (CVSR), provides dense task-aligned feedback by linking reasoning steps to visual evidence, ensuring coherent cross-view entity resolution, and guiding the model toward correct final predictions. Experiments on E2W show that CoRL consistently surpasses strong proprietary and open-source baselines on both reasoning and perception-grounding metrics, while ablations further confirm the necessity of each CVSR component. Beyond that, CoRL generalizes to external spatial reasoning benchmarks and enables effective real-world multi-robot manipulation with calibrated multi-camera rigs, demonstrating cross-view localization and successful grasp-and-place execution. Together, E2W and CoRL provide a principled foundation for learning world-centric scene understanding from distributed, ego-centric observations, advancing collaborative embodied AI.

</details>


### [185] [Surgical Robot, Path Planning, Joint Space, Riemannian Manifolds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14852)
*Yoshiki Yamamoto,Maina Sogabe,Shunichi Hirahara,Toshiki Kaisaki,Tetsuro Miyazaki,Kenji Kawashima*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposed Riemannian manifold-based path planning in joint space to overcome robotic surgery limitations like insertion port constraints and computational complexity of non-concave abdominal surfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic surgery faces challenges: movement restricted around fixed insertion ports, frequent angle limitations during operation, and computational expense of path planning on non-concave abdominal surfaces.

Method: Transform position space into Riemannian manifold for joint space path planning. Define edge cost function to search for desired paths in joint space while reducing joint range of motion. Leverage gradient descent for optimization on mostly non-concave organ surfaces.

Result: Experimental results show the proposed method reduces range of joint angle movement compared to calculations in position space.

Conclusion: Riemannian manifold-based joint space path planning effectively addresses robotic surgery constraints by reducing joint motion range and computational complexity for non-concave abdominal surfaces.

Abstract: Robotic surgery for minimally invasive surgery can reduce the surgeon's workload by autonomously guiding robotic forceps. Movement of the robot is restricted around a fixed insertion port. The robot often encounters angle limitations during operation. Also, the surface of the abdominal cavity is non-concave, making it computationally expensive to find the desired path.In this work, to solve these problems, we propose a method for path planning in joint space by transforming the position into a Riemannian manifold. An edge cost function is defined to search for a desired path in the joint space and reduce the range of motion of the joints. We found that the organ is mostly non-concave, making it easy to find the optimal path using gradient descent method. Experimental results demonstrated that the proposed method reduces the range of joint angle movement compared to calculations in position space.

</details>


### [186] [ViSA: Visited-State Augmentation for Generalized Goal-Space Contrastive Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14887)
*Issa Nakamura,Tomoya Yamanokuchi,Yuki Kadokawa,Jia Qu,Shun Otsub,Ken Miyamoto,Shotaro Miwa,Takamitsu Matsubara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ViSA proposes a data augmentation approach for Contrastive RL to improve goal-space generalization by augmenting hard-to-visit states and learning consistent embedding space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CRL suffers from limited goal-space generalization because it only uses visited states as pseudo-goals during learning, making value function estimation accurate only for easily reachable goals.

Method: Two-component approach: 1) Generate augmented state samples for hard-to-visit states during on-policy exploration, 2) Learn consistent embedding space using augmented states as auxiliary information to regularize embeddings via mutual information-based objective reformulation.

Result: Improved goal-space generalization enabling accurate value estimation for hard-to-visit goals, validated in both simulation and real-world robotic tasks.

Conclusion: ViSA addresses CRL's limitation in goal-space generalization through novel data augmentation and embedding regularization, enhancing value estimation for challenging goals.

Abstract: Goal-Conditioned Reinforcement Learning (GCRL) is a framework for learning a policy that can reach arbitrarily given goals. In particular, Contrastive Reinforcement Learning (CRL) provides a framework for policy updates using an approximation of the value function estimated via contrastive learning, achieving higher sample efficiency compared to conventional methods. However, since CRL treats the visited state as a pseudo-goal during learning, it can accurately estimate the value function only for limited goals. To address this issue, we propose a novel data augmentation approach for CRL called ViSA (Visited-State Augmentation). ViSA consists of two components: 1) generating augmented state samples, with the aim of augmenting hard-to-visit state samples during on-policy exploration, and 2) learning consistent embedding space, which uses an augmented state as auxiliary information to regularize the embedding space by reformulating the objective function of the embedding space based on mutual information. We evaluate ViSA in simulation and real-world robotic tasks and show improved goal-space generalization, which permits accurate value estimation for hard-to-visit goals. Further details can be found on the project page: \href{https://issa-n.github.io/projectPage_ViSA/}{\texttt{https://issa-n.github.io/projectPage\_ViSA/}}

</details>


### [187] [From Folding Mechanics to Robotic Function: A Unified Modeling Framework for Compliant Origami](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14900)
*Bohan Zhang,Bo Wang,Huajiang Ouyang,Zhigang Wu,Haohao Bi,Jiawei Xu,Mingchao Liu,Weicheng Huang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A discrete differential geometry framework unifies rigid folding, elastic deformation, and stability transitions in compliant origami for programmable robotic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing mechanics frameworks lack seamless integration of rigid folding, elastic deformation, and stability-driven transitions in compliant origami, limiting the development of programmable robotic systems.

Method: Developed a geometry-consistent modeling framework based on discrete differential geometry (DDG) that unifies panel elasticity and crease rotation within a single variational formulation, using mid-edge geometric discretization to embed crease-panel coupling.

Result: The framework captures rigid folding limits, distributed bending, multistability, and nonlinear dynamic snap-through within one mechanically consistent structure, enabling programmable control of stability and deformation across rigid and compliant regimes.

Conclusion: Discrete differential geometry serves as a foundational design language for intelligent origami robotics, enabling predictive modeling, stability programming, and mechanics-guided robotic actuation within a unified computational platform.

Abstract: Origami inspired architectures offer a powerful route toward lightweight, reconfigurable, and programmable robotic systems. Yet, a unified mechanics framework capable of seamlessly bridging rigid folding, elastic deformation, and stability driven transitions in compliant origami remains lacking. Here, we introduce a geometry consistent modeling framework based on discrete differential geometry (DDG) that unifies panel elasticity and crease rotation within a single variational formulation. By embedding crease panel coupling directly into a mid edge geometric discretization, the framework naturally captures rigid folding limits, distributed bending, multistability, and nonlinear dynamic snap through within one mechanically consistent structure. This unified description enables programmable control of stability and deformation across rigid and compliant regimes, allowing origami structures to transition from static folding mechanisms to active robotic modules. An implicit dynamic formulation incorporating gravity, contact, friction, and magnetic actuation further supports strongly coupled multiphysics simulations. Through representative examples spanning single fold bifurcation, deployable Miura membranes, bistable Waterbomb modules, and Kresling based crawling robots, we demonstrate how geometry driven mechanics directly informs robotic functionality. This work establishes discrete differential geometry as a foundational design language for intelligent origami robotics, enabling predictive modeling, stability programming, and mechanics guided robotic actuation within a unified computational platform.

</details>


### [188] [PerlAD: Towards Enhanced Closed-loop End-to-end Autonomous Driving with Pseudo-simulation-based Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14908)
*Yinfeng Gao,Qichao Zhang,Deqing Liu,Zhongpu Xia,Guang Li,Kun Ma,Guang Chen,Hangjun Ye,Long Chen,Da-Wei Ding,Dongbin Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PerlAD introduces a pseudo-simulation-based RL method for closed-loop end-to-end autonomous driving that uses offline datasets to create rendering-free training in vector space, combining prediction world models and hierarchical planning to achieve state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation Learning (IL) policies struggle in closed-loop execution due to misalignment between open-loop training objectives and real driving requirements. Reinforcement Learning (RL) offers a solution but faces rendering gaps and high computational costs in simulation-based training environments.

Method: PerlAD constructs a pseudo-simulation in vector space using offline datasets, enabling rendering-free trial-and-error training. It introduces a prediction world model that generates reactive agent trajectories conditioned on ego vehicle plans, and uses a hierarchical decoupled planner combining IL for lateral path generation and RL for longitudinal speed optimization.

Result: PerlAD achieves state-of-the-art performance on the Bench2Drive benchmark, surpassing the previous E2E RL method by 10.29% in Driving Score without requiring expensive online interactions. Additional evaluations on the DOS benchmark confirm its reliability in handling safety-critical occlusion scenarios.

Conclusion: The pseudo-simulation-based RL approach effectively addresses limitations of both IL and traditional RL methods by enabling efficient, rendering-free training in vector space while maintaining closed-loop performance, demonstrating superior driving capabilities without costly online interactions.

Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving policies based on Imitation Learning (IL) often struggle in closed-loop execution due to the misalignment between inadequate open-loop training objectives and real driving requirements. While Reinforcement Learning (RL) offers a solution by directly optimizing driving goals via reward signals, the rendering-based training environments introduce the rendering gap and are inefficient due to high computational costs. To overcome these challenges, we present a novel Pseudo-simulation-based RL method for closed-loop end-to-end autonomous driving, PerlAD. Based on offline datasets, PerlAD constructs a pseudo-simulation that operates in vector space, enabling efficient, rendering-free trial-and-error training. To bridge the gap between static datasets and dynamic closed-loop environments, PerlAD introduces a prediction world model that generates reactive agent trajectories conditioned on the ego vehicle's plan. Furthermore, to facilitate efficient planning, PerlAD utilizes a hierarchical decoupled planner that combines IL for lateral path generation and RL for longitudinal speed optimization. Comprehensive experimental results demonstrate that PerlAD achieves state-of-the-art performance on the Bench2Drive benchmark, surpassing the previous E2E RL method by 10.29% in Driving Score without requiring expensive online interactions. Additional evaluations on the DOS benchmark further confirm its reliability in handling safety-critical occlusion scenarios.

</details>


### [189] [Learning from Mistakes: Post-Training for Driving VLA with Takeover Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14972)
*Yinfeng Gao,Deqing Liu,Qichao Zhang,Yupeng Zheng,Haochen Tian,Guang Li,Hangjun Ye,Long Chen,Da-Wei Ding,Dongbin Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TakeVLA is a novel Vision-Language-Action post-training framework for autonomous driving that addresses distribution shift through pre-takeover language supervision and scenario dreaming reinforcement fine-tuning, achieving state-of-the-art performance on Bench2Drive.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLA paradigms in autonomous driving rely on offline training from static datasets, making them vulnerable to distribution shift. Existing post-training methods using takeover data have limitations: supervision is restricted to post-takeover periods (limiting safety margins) and passive preference optimization lacks active exploration for optimal performance.

Method: TakeVLA introduces two complementary innovations: 1) Pre-takeover language supervision that allows the VLA to learn from mistakes proactively by teaching the model what to do in error-prone situations, cultivating a precautionary mindset that anticipates hazards early. 2) Scenario Dreaming, a reinforcement fine-tuning paradigm that operates in reconstructed takeover scenarios, encouraging active exploration beyond mere preference fitting.

Result: Experiments on the Bench2Drive benchmark demonstrate that TakeVLA achieves state-of-the-art closed-loop performance, surpassing the strong VLA baseline SimLingo by 4.93 in driving score, with enhanced safety margin evidenced by an 11.76% increase in average Time-to-Collision (TTC).

Conclusion: TakeVLA effectively addresses the limitations of current VLA post-training methods by combining proactive learning from mistakes through pre-takeover supervision with active exploration via scenario dreaming, resulting in improved safety margins and overall driving performance.

Abstract: Current Vision-Language-Action (VLA) paradigms in end-to-end autonomous driving rely on offline training from static datasets, leaving them vulnerable to distribution shift. Recent post-training methods use takeover data to mitigate this by augmenting the dataset with high-quality expert takeover samples, yet they suffer from two key limitations: supervision restricted to the period after the takeover moments leads to policies with limited safety margins, and passive preference optimization lacks active exploration for optimal performance. In this paper, we propose TakeVLA, a novel VLA post-training framework that overcomes these shortcomings through two complementary innovations. First, we introduce pre-takeover language supervision, which allows the VLA to learn from mistakes proactively. By explicitly teaching the model about what to do in error-prone situations, we cultivate a precautionary mindset that anticipates hazards early and substantially enlarges safety margins. Second, we propose Scenario Dreaming, a reinforcement fine-tuning paradigm that operates in reconstruceted takeover scenarios, encouraging active exploration beyond mere preference fitting. Experiments on the Bench2Drive benchmark demonstrate that TakeVLA achieves state-of-the-art closed-loop performance, surpassing the strong VLA baseline SimLingo by 4.93 in driving score, with an enhanced safety margin as evidenced by an 11.76% increase in average TTC.

</details>


### [190] [ReMAP-DP: Reprojected Multi-view Aligned PointMaps for Diffusion Policy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14977)
*Xinzhang Yang,Renjun Wu,Jinyan Liu,Xuesong Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ReMAP-DP is a novel robot policy framework that combines standardized perspective reprojection with a dual-stream diffusion architecture to integrate 2D semantic reasoning with explicit 3D spatial awareness for high-precision manipulation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generalist robot policies based on 2D visual representations lack explicit 3D spatial awareness needed for high-precision tasks, while existing 3D integration methods struggle with sparse point cloud irregularity and geometric distortion from multi-view orthographic rendering.

Method: ReMAP-DP synergizes standardized perspective reprojection with a structure-aware dual-stream diffusion policy. It couples re-projected views with pixel-aligned PointMaps, using learnable modality embeddings to fuse frozen semantic features and explicit geometric descriptors for precise implicit patch-level alignment.

Result: On RoboTwin 2.0, ReMAP-DP achieves 59.3% average success rate, outperforming DP3 baseline by +6.6%. On ManiSkill 3, it yields 28% improvement over DP3 on geometrically challenging Stack Cube task. The method exhibits remarkable real-world robustness and superior data efficiency from few demonstrations.

Conclusion: ReMAP-DP successfully bridges the gap between 2D semantic reasoning and 3D spatial awareness for high-precision manipulation, demonstrating superior performance, robustness, and data efficiency across simulation and real-world environments.

Abstract: Generalist robot policies built upon 2D visual representations excel at semantic reasoning but inherently lack the explicit 3D spatial awareness required for high-precision tasks. Existing 3D integration methods struggle to bridge this gap due to the structural irregularity of sparse point clouds and the geometric distortion introduced by multi-view orthographic rendering. To overcome these barriers, we present ReMAP-DP, a novel framework synergizing standardized perspective reprojection with a structure-aware dual-stream diffusion policy. By coupling the re-projected views with pixel-aligned PointMaps, our dual-stream architecture leverages learnable modality embeddings to fuse frozen semantic features and explicit geometric descriptors, ensuring precise implicit patch-level alignment. Extensive experiments across simulation and real-world environments demonstrate ReMAP-DP's superior performance in diverse manipulation tasks. On RoboTwin 2.0, it attains a 59.3% average success rate, outperforming the DP3 baseline by +6.6%. On ManiSkill 3, our method yields a 28% improvement over DP3 on the geometrically challenging Stack Cube task. Furthermore, ReMAP-DP exhibits remarkable real-world robustness, executing high-precision and dynamic manipulations with superior data efficiency from only a handful of demonstrations. Project page is available at: https://icr-lab.github.io/ReMAP-DP/

</details>


### [191] [CycleRL: Sim-to-Real Deep Reinforcement Learning for Robust Autonomous Bicycle Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15013)
*Gelu Liu,Teng Wang,Zhijie Wu,Junliang Wu,Songyuan Li,Xiangwei Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CycleRL is a sim-to-real deep reinforcement learning framework for autonomous bicycle control that trains neural policies in simulation with domain randomization for direct hardware transfer, achieving high balance success and low tracking errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional control strategies for autonomous bicycles struggle with underactuated nonlinear dynamics, sensitivity to model mismatches, and limited adaptability to real-world uncertainties, necessitating a more robust approach.

Method: End-to-end neural control policy trained in NVIDIA Isaac Sim using Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with composite reward function for balance, velocity tracking, and steering control, plus systematic domain randomization for sim-to-real transfer.

Result: 99.90% balance success rate, 1.15° steering tracking error, 0.18 m/s velocity tracking error in simulation, with successful hardware transfer demonstrating real-world applicability.

Conclusion: Deep reinforcement learning is an effective paradigm for autonomous bicycle control, offering superior adaptability over traditional methods, validated by both simulation performance and successful real-world implementation.

Abstract: Autonomous bicycles offer a promising agile solution for urban mobility and last-mile logistics, however, conventional control strategies often struggle with their underactuated nonlinear dynamics, suffering from sensitivity to model mismatches and limited adaptability to real-world uncertainties. To address this, this paper presents CycleRL, the first sim-to-real deep reinforcement learning framework designed for robust autonomous bicycle control. Our approach trains an end-to-end neural control policy within the high-fidelity NVIDIA Isaac Sim environment, leveraging Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) to circumvent the need for an explicit dynamics model. The framework features a composite reward function tailored for concurrent balance maintenance, velocity tracking, and steering control. Crucially, systematic domain randomization is employed to bridge the simulation-to-reality gap and facilitate direct transfer. In simulation, CycleRL achieves considerable performance, including a 99.90% balance success rate, a low steering tracking error of 1.15°, and a velocity tracking error of 0.18 m/s. These quantitative results, coupled with successful hardware transfer, validate DRL as an effective paradigm for autonomous bicycle control, offering superior adaptability over traditional methods. Video demonstrations are available at https://anony6f05.github.io/CycleRL/.

</details>


### [192] [AnoleVLA: Lightweight Vision-Language-Action Model with Deep State Space Models for Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15046)
*Yusuke Takagi,Motonari Kambara,Daichi Yashima,Koki Seno,Kento Tokura,Komei Sugiura*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AnoleVLA is a lightweight Vision-Language-Action model using deep state space models for efficient robotic manipulation from visual and language inputs, achieving higher success rates and faster inference than larger VLAs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-guided robotic manipulation is essential for service robots but faces deployment challenges due to computational costs of standard transformer-based VLAs in resource-constrained environments.

Method: Proposes AnoleVLA, a lightweight VLA that uses deep state space models to efficiently process multimodal sequences (visual and textual inputs) for trajectory generation.

Result: In real-world evaluations, AnoleVLA outperformed a representative large-scale VLA by 21 points in task success rate while achieving approximately 3x faster inference speed.

Conclusion: Deep state space models enable efficient multimodal sequence processing for robotic manipulation, offering superior performance and speed compared to standard transformer backbones in resource-constrained environments.

Abstract: In this study, we address the problem of language-guided robotic manipulation, where a robot is required to manipulate a wide range of objects based on visual observations and natural language instructions. This task is essential for service robots that operate in human environments, and requires safety, efficiency, and task-level generality. Although Vision-Language-Action models (VLAs) have demonstrated strong performance for this task, their deployment in resource-constrained environments remains challenging because of the computational cost of standard transformer backbones. To overcome this limitation, we propose AnoleVLA, a lightweight VLA that uses a deep state space model to process multimodal sequences efficiently. The model leverages its lightweight and fast sequential state modeling to process visual and textual inputs, which allows the robot to generate trajectories efficiently. We evaluated the proposed method in both simulation and physical experiments. Notably, in real-world evaluations, AnoleVLA outperformed a representative large-scale VLA by 21 points for the task success rate while achieving an inference speed approximately three times faster.

</details>


### [193] [Multi-Mode Pneumatic Artificial Muscles Driven by Hybrid Positive-Negative Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15066)
*Siyuan Feng,Ruoyu Feng,Shuguang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: IN-FOAMs are inflatable fluid-driven origami-inspired artificial muscles made from low-cost heat-sealable materials, capable of multiple motion modes (contracting, bending, twisting, rotating) through programmable skeleton patterns and hybrid positive-negative pressure control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop flexible, lightweight, and portable artificial muscles that can mimic lifelike robotic movements for applications in flexible wearable devices and compact soft robotic systems.

Method: Manufactured using low-cost heat-sealable sheet materials through heat-pressing and heat-sealing processes; features inflatable skeleton enclosed within outer skin driven by hybrid positive-negative pressure; includes programmable skeleton patterns, multilayer structures for enhanced contraction, and multi-channel approach for integrated motion modes.

Result: IN-FOAMs achieve tunable output force and contraction through multiple operation modes with hybrid pressure control; demonstrate various motions (contracting, bending, twisting, rotating); multilayer structures enhance contraction ratio; multi-channel skeletons enable integration of multiple motion modes in single unit.

Conclusion: IN-FOAMs represent a promising architecture for artificial muscles with great potential for flexible wearable devices and compact soft robotic systems due to their programmability, tunability, and manufacturing simplicity.

Abstract: Artificial muscles embody human aspirations for engineering lifelike robotic movements. This paper introduces an architecture for Inflatable Fluid-Driven Origami-Inspired Artificial Muscles (IN-FOAMs). A typical IN-FOAM consists of an inflatable skeleton enclosed within an outer skin, which can be driven using a combination of positive and negative pressures (e.g., compressed air and vacuum). IN-FOAMs are manufactured using low-cost heat-sealable sheet materials through heat-pressing and heat-sealing processes. Thus, they can be ultra-thin when not actuated, making them flexible, lightweight, and portable. The skeleton patterns are programmable, enabling a variety of motions, including contracting, bending, twisting, and rotating, based on specific skeleton designs. We conducted comprehensive experimental, theoretical, and numerical studies to investigate IN-FOAM's basic mechanical behavior and properties. The results show that IN-FOAM's output force and contraction can be tuned through multiple operation modes with the applied hybrid positive-negative pressure. Additionally, we propose multilayer skeleton structures to enhance the contraction ratio further, and we demonstrate a multi-channel skeleton approach that allows the integration of multiple motion modes into a single IN-FOAM. These findings indicate that IN-FOAMs hold great potential for future applications in flexible wearable devices and compact soft robotic systems.

</details>


### [194] [HALO:Closing Sim-to-Real Gap for Heavy-loaded Humanoid Agile Motion Skills via Differentiable Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15084)
*Xingyi Wang,Chenyun Zhang,Weiji Xie,Chao Yu,Wei Song,Chenjia Bai,Shiqiang Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Two-stage gradient-based system ID framework using MuJoCo XLA for humanoid robots carrying unknown payloads, enabling zero-shot policy transfer to hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots in real-world scenarios often carry unknown payloads, causing significant model mismatch that degrades simulation-to-reality reinforcement learning effectiveness.

Method: Two-stage gradient-based system identification framework built on differentiable simulator MuJoCo XLA: first stage calibrates nominal robot model using real-world data to reduce intrinsic sim-to-real discrepancies; second stage identifies mass distribution of unknown payload.

Result: Extensive simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate more precise parameter identification, improved motion tracking accuracy, and substantially enhanced agility and robustness compared to existing baselines.

Conclusion: By explicitly reducing structured model bias prior to policy training, the approach enables zero-shot transfer of reinforcement learning policies to hardware under heavy-load conditions.

Abstract: Humanoid robots deployed in real-world scenarios often need to carry unknown payloads, which introduce significant mismatch and degrade the effectiveness of simulation-to-reality reinforcement learning methods. To address this challenge, we propose a two-stage gradient-based system identification framework built on the differentiable simulator MuJoCo XLA. The first stage calibrates the nominal robot model using real-world data to reduce intrinsic sim-to-real discrepancies, while the second stage further identifies the mass distribution of the unknown payload. By explicitly reducing structured model bias prior to policy training, our approach enables zero-shot transfer of reinforcement learning policies to hardware under heavy-load conditions. Extensive simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate more precise parameter identification, improved motion tracking accuracy, and substantially enhanced agility and robustness compared to existing baselines. Project Page: https://mwondering.github.io/halo-humanoid/

</details>


### [195] [AeroGrab: A Unified Framework for Aerial Grasping in Cluttered Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15097)
*Shivansh Pratap Singh,Naveen Sudheer Nair,Samaksh Ujjawal,Sarthak Mishra,Soham Patil,Rishabh Dev Yadav,Spandan Roy*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Integrated pipeline for aerial grasping in cluttered environments combining active exploration, 6-DoF grasp generation, collision-aware feasibility evaluation, and language instruction processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing aerial manipulation systems have limitations: they rely on centroid-based grasping, lack integration between grasp generation, active exploration, and language task specification, and don't form complete end-to-end systems. Challenges include occlusions and collision risks in cluttered environments.

Method: 1) Process language instruction to identify target object; 2) Active exploration to gain better views of object; 3) 6-DoF grasp candidate generation network for each view; 4) Collision-aware feasibility framework to evaluate candidates; 5) Selection of overall best grasp; 6) Execution using standard trajectory generation and control methods.

Result: Experiments in cluttered real-world scenarios demonstrate robust and reliable grasp execution, showing effectiveness of combining active perception with feasibility-aware grasp selection for aerial manipulation.

Conclusion: The integrated pipeline successfully addresses limitations of existing systems by combining language instruction processing, active exploration, 6-DoF grasp generation, and collision-aware feasibility evaluation, enabling reliable aerial grasping in cluttered environments.

Abstract: Reliable aerial grasping in cluttered environments remains challenging due to occlusions and collision risks. Existing aerial manipulation pipelines largely rely on centroid-based grasping and lack integration between the grasp pose generation models, active exploration, and language-level task specification, resulting in the absence of a complete end-to-end system. In this work, we present an integrated pipeline for reliable aerial grasping in cluttered environments. Given a scene and a language instruction, the system identifies the target object and actively explores it to gain better views of the object. During exploration, a grasp generation network predicts multiple 6-DoF grasp candidates for each view. Each candidate is evaluated using a collision-aware feasibility framework, and the overall best grasp is selected and executed using standard trajectory generation and control methods. Experiments in cluttered real-world scenarios demonstrate robust and reliable grasp execution, highlighting the effectiveness of combining active perception with feasibility-aware grasp selection for aerial manipulation.

</details>


### [196] [BodyGuards: Escorting by Multiple Robots in Unknown Environment under Limited Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15108)
*Zhuoli Tian,Yanze Bao,Meng Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BodyGuards is a multi-robot escorting framework that protects a human operator in adversarial environments through task-oriented exploration, dynamic risk mapping, and coordinated robot strategies to minimize operator navigation time while ensuring safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Protecting human operators in high-risk missions (reconnaissance, disaster response, subterranean operations) when communication is restricted, moving beyond complete coverage approaches to focus on task-oriented exploration that minimizes operator navigation time while ensuring safety against adversarial threats.

Method: Three-component framework: (I) Dynamic operator movement strategy with local risk map for proactive path planning; (II) Dual-mode robotic strategy combining frontier-based exploration with optimized return events for threat detection and intermittent communication; (III) Multi-robot coordination protocols for joint exploration and information sharing.

Result: Extensive human-in-the-loop simulations and hardware experiments show significant reduction in operator risk and mission time, outperforming baselines in adversarial and constrained environments.

Conclusion: BodyGuards successfully integrates collaborative exploration, communication, and escorting to protect human operators in high-risk missions, demonstrating superior performance in minimizing navigation time while ensuring safety under adversarial conditions.

Abstract: Multi-robot systems are increasingly deployed in high-risk missions such as reconnaissance, disaster response, and subterranean operations. Protecting a human operator while navigating unknown and adversarial environments remains a critical challenge, especially when the communication among the operator and robots is restricted. Unlike existing collaborative exploration methods that aim for complete coverage, this work focuses on task-oriented exploration to minimize the navigation time of the operator to reach its goal while ensuring safety under adversarial threats. A novel escorting framework BodyGuards, is proposed to explicitly integrate seamlessly collaborative exploration, inter-robot-operator communication and escorting. The framework consists of three core components: (I) a dynamic movement strategy for the operator that maintains a local map with risk zones for proactive path planning; (II) a dual-mode robotic strategy combining frontier based exploration with optimized return events to balance exploration, threat detection, and intermittent communication; and (III) multi-robot coordination protocols that jointly plan exploration and information sharing for efficient escorting. Extensive human-in-the-loop simulations and hardware experiments demonstrate that the method significantly reduces operator risk and mission time, outperforming baselines in adversarial and constrained environments.

</details>


### [197] [A Novel Camera-to-Robot Calibration Method for Vision-Based Floor Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15126)
*Jan Andre Rudolph,Dennis Haitz,Markus Ulrich*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Novel hand-eye calibration method for ground-observing mobile robots using laser tracker and camera with custom referencing plate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cameras on mobile robots are rarely used for ground-observing measurement tasks despite being common, while laser trackers provide precise localization but need integration with camera systems.

Method: Design referencing plate with reflector nests for laser tracker pose acquisition and camera calibration target; procedure involves estimating plate pose, plate-camera pose, robot pose, then computing robot-camera transformation.

Result: Experiments demonstrate sub-millimeter repeatability in calibration accuracy.

Conclusion: Proposed method successfully integrates laser tracker 3D metrology with camera-based 2D imaging for precise hand-eye calibration of ground-observing mobile robots.

Abstract: A novel hand-eye calibration method for ground-observing mobile robots is proposed. While cameras on mobile robots are com- mon, they are rarely used for ground-observing measurement tasks. Laser trackers are increasingly used in robotics for precise localization. A referencing plate is designed to combine the two measurement modalities of laser-tracker 3D metrology and camera- based 2D imaging. It incorporates reflector nests for pose acquisition using a laser tracker and a camera calibration target that is observed by the robot-mounted camera. The procedure comprises estimating the plate pose, the plate-camera pose, and the robot pose, followed by computing the robot-camera transformation. Experiments indicate sub-millimeter repeatability.

</details>


### [198] [Confusion-Aware In-Context-Learning for Vision-Language Models in Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15134)
*Yayun He,Zuheng Kang,Botao Zhao,Zhouyin Wu,Junqing Peng,Jianzong Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CAICL addresses VLM shortcut learning in robotic manipulation by localizing confusion sources and using them as prompts to focus on discriminative features, achieving 85.5% success on VIMA-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models in robotic manipulation suffer from lack of robustness and unpredictable errors in confusable object scenarios due to shortcut learning problems inherent in VLMs, limiting their ability to accurately distinguish between confusable features.

Method: Confusion-Aware In-Context Learning (CAICL) begins with confusion localization and analysis to identify potential sources of confusion, then uses this information as a prompt for the VLM to focus on features most likely to cause misidentification.

Result: Extensive experiments on VIMA-Bench show CAICL effectively addresses shortcut learning, achieving 85.5% success rate and demonstrating good stability across tasks with different degrees of generalization.

Conclusion: CAICL enhances VLM performance in confusable scenarios for robotic manipulation by explicitly addressing shortcut learning through confusion-aware prompting, providing a robust solution for handling confusable objects.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have significantly improved the generalization capabilities of robotic manipulation. However, VLM-based systems often suffer from a lack of robustness, leading to unpredictable errors, particularly in scenarios involving confusable objects. Our preliminary analysis reveals that these failures are mainly caused by shortcut learning problem inherently in VLMs, limiting their ability to accurately distinguish between confusable features. To this end, we propose Confusion-Aware In-Context Learning (CAICL), a method that enhances VLM performance in confusable scenarios for robotic manipulation. The approach begins with confusion localization and analysis, identifying potential sources of confusion. This information is then used as a prompt for the VLM to focus on features most likely to cause misidentification. Extensive experiments on the VIMA-Bench show that CAICL effectively addresses the shortcut learning issue, achieving a 85.5\% success rate and showing good stability across tasks with different degrees of generalization.

</details>


### [199] [Master Micro Residual Correction with Adaptive Tactile Fusion and Force-Mixed Control for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15152)
*Xingting Li,Yifan Xie,Han Liu,Wei Hou,Guangyu Chen,Shoujie Li,Wenbo Ding*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: M2-ResiPolicy: A Master-Micro residual control architecture combining high-level diffusion-based planning (10Hz) with low-level reactive correction (60Hz) using tactile feedback for contact-rich manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current visual imitation learning methods fail to perceive critical interaction cues like friction variations or incipient slip, and struggle to balance global task coherence with local reactive feedback in contact-rich manipulation tasks.

Method: Two-level architecture: Master-Guidance Policy (MGP) at 10Hz with diffusion-based backbone and tactile-intensity-driven adaptive fusion for vision/touch weighting; Micro-Residual Corrector (MRC) at 60Hz using lightweight GRU for real-time action compensation based on TCP wrench feedback; integrated with force-mixed PBIC execution layer for contact force regulation.

Result: Significantly outperforms standard Diffusion Policy (DP) and state-of-the-art Reactive Diffusion Policy (RDP), achieving 93% damage-free success rate in chip grasping and superior force regulation stability across fragile object grasping and precision insertion tasks.

Conclusion: M2-ResiPolicy effectively addresses the multi-timescale control challenge in contact-rich manipulation by synergizing high-level action guidance with low-level reactive correction, demonstrating superior performance in delicate manipulation tasks requiring both global planning and local feedback.

Abstract: Robotic contact-rich and fine-grained manipulation remains a significant challenge due to complex interaction dynamics and the competing requirements of multi-timescale control. While current visual imitation learning methods excel at long-horizon planning, they often fail to perceive critical interaction cues like friction variations or incipient slip, and struggle to balance global task coherence with local reactive feedback. To address these challenges, we propose M2-ResiPolicy, a novel Master-Micro residual control architecture that synergizes high-level action guidance with low-level correction. The framework consists of a Master-Guidance Policy (MGP) operating at 10 Hz, which generates temporally consistent action chunks via a diffusion-based backbone and employs a tactile-intensity-driven adaptive fusion mechanism to dynamically modulate perceptual weights between vision and touch. Simultaneously, a high-frequency (60 Hz) Micro-Residual Corrector (MRC) utilizes a lightweight GRU to provide real-time action compensation based on TCP wrench feedback. This policy is further integrated with a force-mixed PBIC execution layer, effectively regulating contact forces to ensure interaction safety. Experiments across several demanding tasks including fragile object grasping and precision insertion, demonstrate that M2-ResiPolicy significantly outperforms standard Diffusion Policy (DP) and state-of-the-art Reactive Diffusion Policy (RDP), achieving a 93\% damage-free success rate in chip grasping and superior force regulation stability.

</details>


### [200] [ForceVLA2: Unleashing Hybrid Force-Position Control with Force Awareness for Contact-Rich Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15169)
*Yang Li,Zhaxizhuoma,Hongru Jiang,Junjie Xia,Hongquan Zhang,Jinda Du,Yunsong Zhou,Jia Zeng,Ce Hao,Jieji Ren,Qiaojun Yu,Cewu Lu,Yu Qiao,Jiangmiao Pang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ForceVLA2 is an end-to-end vision-language-action framework that integrates hybrid force-position control and explicit force awareness for contact-rich manipulation tasks, outperforming baseline methods by significant margins.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current embodied intelligence for contact-rich manipulation relies heavily on position control with limited explicit awareness and regulation of interaction forces, which restricts stability, precision, and robustness in real-world tasks.

Method: ForceVLA2 introduces force-based prompts into the VLM expert to construct force-aware task concepts, and employs a Cross-Scale Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) in the action expert to adaptively fuse these concepts with real-time interaction forces for closed-loop hybrid force-position regulation.

Result: ForceVLA2 substantially improves success rates and reliability in contact-rich manipulation, outperforming pi0 and pi0.5 by 48.0% and 35.0% respectively across 5 tasks, while mitigating common failure modes like arm overload and unstable contact.

Conclusion: ForceVLA2 advances force-aware interactive physical intelligence in VLAs by integrating explicit force awareness with hybrid control, demonstrating significant performance improvements in contact-rich manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Embodied intelligence for contact-rich manipulation has predominantly relied on position control, while explicit awareness and regulation of interaction forces remain under-explored, limiting stability, precision, and robustness in real-world tasks. We propose ForceVLA2, an end-to-end vision-language-action framework that equips robots with hybrid force-position control and explicit force awareness. ForceVLA2 introduces force-based prompts into the VLM expert to construct force-aware task concepts across stages, and employs a Cross-Scale Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) in the action expert to adaptively fuse these concepts with real-time interaction forces for closed-loop hybrid force-position regulation. To support learning and evaluation, we construct ForceVLA2-Dataset, containing 1,000 trajectories over 5 contact-rich tasks, including wiping, pressing, and assembling, with multi-view images, task prompts, proprioceptive state, and force signals. Extensive experiments show that ForceVLA2 substantially improves success rates and reliability in contact-rich manipulation, outperforming pi0 and pi0.5 by 48.0% and 35.0%, respectively, across the 5 tasks, and mitigating common failure modes such as arm overload and unstable contact, thereby actively advancing force-aware interactive physical intelligence in VLAs. The project page is available at https://sites.google.com/view/force-vla2/home.

</details>


### [201] [KiRAS: Keyframe Guided Self-Imitation for Robust and Adaptive Skill Learning in Quadruped Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15179)
*Xiaoyi Wei,Peng Zhai,Jiaxin Tu,Yueqi Zhang,Yuqi Li,Zonghao Zhang,Hu Zhou,Lihua Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: KiRAS is a framework that uses keyframe-guided self-imitation to learn diverse quadruped robot skills without expert datasets, then adapts them to complex terrains while preventing catastrophic forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multi-skill policies for quadruped robots lack generalization to unstructured environments due to limited datasets on complex terrains, requiring a method to integrate terrain adaptability with skill diversity without extensive expert demonstrations.

Method: Keyframe Guided Self-Imitation for Robust and Adaptive Skill Learning (KiRAS): 1) Learns diverse skills on flat terrain through keyframe-guided self-imitation (no expert datasets needed), 2) Continues training same policy on rough terrains for robustness, 3) Uses proficiency-based Skill Initialization Technique to prevent catastrophic forgetting.

Result: Experiments on Solo-8 and Unitree Go1 robots demonstrate robust skill acquisition and smooth transitions across challenging terrains, showing the framework's effectiveness as a lightweight platform for multi-skill generation and dataset collection.

Conclusion: KiRAS enables flexible skill transitions that enhance locomotion on challenging terrains by using keyframes as universal skill representations, relaxing dataset constraints while maintaining skill diversity and terrain adaptability.

Abstract: With advances in reinforcement learning and imitation learning, quadruped robots can acquire diverse skills within a single policy by imitating multiple skill-specific datasets. However, the lack of datasets on complex terrains limits the ability of such multi-skill policies to generalize effectively in unstructured environments. Inspired by animation, we adopt keyframes as minimal and universal skill representations, relaxing dataset constraints and enabling the integration of terrain adaptability with skill diversity. We propose Keyframe Guided Self-Imitation for Robust and Adaptive Skill Learning (KiRAS), an end-to-end framework for acquiring and transitioning between diverse skill primitives on complex terrains. KiRAS first learns diverse skills on flat terrain through keyframe-guided self-imitation, eliminating the need for expert datasets; then continues training the same policy network on rough terrains to enhance robustness. To eliminate catastrophic forgetting, a proficiency-based Skill Initialization Technique is introduced. Experiments on Solo-8 and Unitree Go1 robots show that KiRAS enables robust skill acquisition and smooth transitions across challenging terrains. This framework demonstrates its potential as a lightweight platform for multi-skill generation and dataset collection. It further enables flexible skill transitions that enhance locomotion on challenging terrains.

</details>


### [202] [What Matters for Scalable and Robust Learning in End-to-End Driving Planners?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15185)
*David Holtz,Niklas Hanselmann,Simon Doll,Marius Cordts,Bernt Schiele*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: BevAD is a lightweight, scalable end-to-end autonomous driving architecture that achieves 72.7% success rate on Bench2Drive through systematic analysis of architectural patterns and pure imitation learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current end-to-end autonomous driving architectures optimized for open-loop performance often fail to translate to robust closed-loop driving. The paper aims to systematically examine architectural patterns' impact on closed-loop performance to develop a more scalable and effective architecture.

Method: Systematic analysis of three architectural patterns: (1) high-resolution perceptual representations, (2) disentangled trajectory representations, and (3) generative planning. Based on insights from this analysis, the authors develop BevAD - a novel lightweight end-to-end driving architecture using pure imitation learning.

Result: BevAD achieves 72.7% success rate on the Bench2Drive benchmark and demonstrates strong data-scaling behavior. The analysis reveals unexpected limitations and underexplored synergies between architectural patterns for closed-loop driving.

Conclusion: Careful architectural design considering the combined impact of perceptual representations, trajectory representations, and planning approaches is crucial for scalable closed-loop autonomous driving. BevAD provides an effective lightweight solution that scales well with data.

Abstract: End-to-end autonomous driving has gained significant attention for its potential to learn robust behavior in interactive scenarios and scale with data. Popular architectures often build on separate modules for perception and planning connected through latent representations, such as bird's eye view feature grids, to maintain end-to-end differentiability. This paradigm emerged mostly on open-loop datasets, with evaluation focusing not only on driving performance, but also intermediate perception tasks. Unfortunately, architectural advances that excel in open-loop often fail to translate to scalable learning of robust closed-loop driving. In this paper, we systematically re-examine the impact of common architectural patterns on closed-loop performance: (1) high-resolution perceptual representations, (2) disentangled trajectory representations, and (3) generative planning. Crucially, our analysis evaluates the combined impact of these patterns, revealing both unexpected limitations as well as underexplored synergies. Building on these insights, we introduce BevAD, a novel lightweight and highly scalable end-to-end driving architecture. BevAD achieves 72.7% success rate on the Bench2Drive benchmark and demonstrates strong data-scaling behavior using pure imitation learning. Our code and models are publicly available here: https://dmholtz.github.io/bevad/

</details>


### [203] [NavGSim: High-Fidelity Gaussian Splatting Simulator for Large-Scale Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15186)
*Jiahang Liu,Yuanxing Duan,Jiazhao Zhang,Minghan Li,Shaoan Wang,Zhizheng Zhang,He Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NavGSim is a Gaussian Splatting-based simulator that generates high-fidelity, large-scale navigation environments for robot learning, featuring photorealistic rendering across expansive scenes and direct collision detection from reconstructed Gaussians.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realistic environment simulation is critical for robot learning, especially in navigation tasks where trajectories span multiple rooms or entire floors. Current simulators face challenges in rendering quality and physical simulation at large scales.

Method: Built on hierarchical 3D Gaussian Splatting framework for photorealistic rendering in large scenes. Introduces Gaussian Splatting-based slice technique for direct extraction of navigable areas from reconstructed Gaussians. Provides comprehensive APIs supporting multi-GPU development, custom scene reconstruction, robot configuration, policy training, and evaluation.

Result: NavGSim enables high-fidelity simulation across scenes spanning hundreds of square meters. When used to train a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model, it significantly enhances scene understanding, enabling effective handling of diverse navigation queries in both simulated and real-world environments.

Conclusion: NavGSim addresses the critical challenge of realistic environment simulation for robot navigation learning by providing a scalable, high-fidelity simulator that improves policy performance through enhanced scene understanding.

Abstract: Simulating realistic environments for robots is widely recognized as a critical challenge in robot learning, particularly in terms of rendering and physical simulation. This challenge becomes even more pronounced in navigation tasks, where trajectories often extend across multiple rooms or entire floors. In this work, we present NavGSim, a Gaussian Splatting-based simulator designed to generate high-fidelity, large-scale navigation environments. Built upon a hierarchical 3D Gaussian Splatting framework, NavGSim enables photorealistic rendering in expansive scenes spanning hundreds of square meters. To simulate navigation collisions, we introduce a Gaussian Splatting-based slice technique that directly extracts navigable areas from reconstructed Gaussians. Additionally, for ease of use, we provide comprehensive NavGSim APIs supporting multi-GPU development, including tools for custom scene reconstruction, robot configuration, policy training, and evaluation. To evaluate NavGSim's effectiveness, we train a Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model using trajectories collected from NavGSim and assess its performance in both simulated and real-world environments. Our results demonstrate that NavGSim significantly enhances the VLA model's scene understanding, enabling the policy to handle diverse navigation queries effectively.

</details>


### [204] [Coupled Particle Filters for Robust Affordance Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15223)
*Patrick Lowin,Vito Mengers,Oliver Brock*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A novel method for robotic affordance estimation that uses two coupled recursive estimators for graspable and movable regions, outperforming existing methods by 200-300% in precision and achieving 70% real-world success rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robotic affordance estimation faces challenges from visual, geometric, and semantic ambiguities in sensory input, requiring methods that can disambiguate these signals effectively.

Method: Proposes a method using two coupled recursive estimators for sub-aspects of affordances: graspable and movable regions. Each estimator encodes property-specific regularities to reduce uncertainty, while their coupling enables bidirectional information exchange that focuses attention on regions where both agree (affordances).

Result: Outperforms three recent affordance estimators (Where2Act, Hands-as-Probes, and HRP) by 308%, 245%, and 257% in precision on a real-world dataset. Remains robust under challenging conditions like low light or cluttered environments. Achieves 70% success rate in real-world evaluation.

Conclusion: Coupling complementary estimators yields precise, robust, and embodiment-appropriate affordance predictions, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach for robotic affordance estimation.

Abstract: Robotic affordance estimation is challenging due to visual, geometric, and semantic ambiguities in sensory input. We propose a method that disambiguates these signals using two coupled recursive estimators for sub-aspects of affordances: graspable and movable regions. Each estimator encodes property-specific regularities to reduce uncertainty, while their coupling enables bidirectional information exchange that focuses attention on regions where both agree, i.e., affordances. Evaluated on a real-world dataset, our method outperforms three recent affordance estimators (Where2Act, Hands-as-Probes, and HRP) by 308%, 245%, and 257% in precision, and remains robust under challenging conditions such as low light or cluttered environments. Furthermore, our method achieves a 70% success rate in our real-world evaluation. These results demonstrate that coupling complementary estimators yields precise, robust, and embodiment-appropriate affordance predictions.

</details>


### [205] [A Methodology for Dynamic Parameters Identification of 3-DOF Parallel Robots in Terms of Relevant Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15254)
*Miguel Díaz-Rodríguez,Vicente Mata,Angel Valera,Alvaro Page*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Proposes a strategy for identifying relevant dynamic parameters in fully parallel robots using model simplification, weighted least squares, and statistical reduction to ensure physical feasibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dynamic parameter identification is crucial for model-based control and realistic simulations, but traditional methods often identify only base parameters, some of which have minimal contribution to dynamics and become poorly identifiable due to measurement noise and modeling discrepancies.

Method: Start from full dynamic model, apply simplifications based on link geometry and leg symmetry in parallel robots, use weighted least squares for identification, then statistically reduce the model while maintaining physical feasibility conditions.

Result: Experimental validation on two different configurations of actual 3-DOF parallel robots shows that identified models' inverse and forward dynamics responses agree with experiments; a method for obtaining forward dynamics in terms of relevant parameters is also proposed.

Conclusion: The proposed strategy successfully identifies relevant dynamic parameters for fully parallel robots, ensuring physical feasibility and providing accurate dynamic models for control and simulation applications.

Abstract: The identification of dynamic parameters in mechanical systems is important for improving model-based control as well as for performing realistic dynamic simulations. Generally, when identification techniques are applied only a subset of so-called base parameters can be identified. More even, some of these parameters cannot be identified properly given that they have a small contribution to the robot dynamics and hence in the presence of noise in measurements and discrepancy in modeling, their quality of being identifiable decreases. For this reason, a strategy for dynamic parameter identification of fully parallel robots in terms of a subset called relevant parameters is put forward. The objective of the proposed methodology is to start from a full dynamic model, then simplification concerning the geometry of each link and, the symmetry due to legs of fully parallel robots, are carried out. After that, the identification is done by Weighted Least Squares. Then, with statistical considerations the model is reduced until the physical feasibility conditions are met. The application of the propose strategy has been experimentally tested on two difierent configurations of actual 3-DOF parallel robots. The response of the inverse and forward dynamics of the identified models agrees with experiments. In order to evaluate the forward dynamics response, an approach for obtaining the forward dynamics in terms of the relevant parameters is also proposed.

</details>


### [206] [HapticVLA: Contact-Rich Manipulation via Vision-Language-Action Model without Inference-Time Tactile Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15257)
*Konstantin Gubernatorov,Mikhail Sannikov,Ilya Mikhalchuk,Egor Kuznetsov,Makar Artemov,Ogunwoye Faith Ouwatobi,Marcelino Fernando,Artem Asanov,Ziang Guo,Dzmitry Tsetserukou*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HapticVLA enables tactile-aware robotic manipulation without requiring on-board tactile sensors at inference by using a two-stage approach: safety-aware flow matching with tactile rewards, followed by tactile token distillation into a vision-language-action model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tactile sensing is essential for dexterous manipulation but dedicated hardware increases costs and reduces reproducibility. The authors aim to enable tactile-aware manipulation that can be learned offline and deployed without direct haptic feedback at inference time.

Method: Two-stage approach: 1) Safety-Aware Reward-Weighted Flow Matching (SA-RWFM) trains a flow-matching action expert using precomputed tactile rewards that penalize excessive force and suboptimal trajectories. 2) Tactile Distillation (TD) transfers tactile awareness into a conventional VLA by distilling a compact tactile token from the SA-RWFM teacher and training a student VLA to predict this token from vision and state inputs.

Result: HapticVLA achieves 86.7% mean success rate in real-world experiments, consistently outperforming baseline VLAs including those provided with direct tactile feedback during inference.

Conclusion: The approach successfully preserves contact-rich tactile-aware reasoning within VLA architectures while eliminating the need for on-board tactile sensors during deployment, demonstrating that tactile-aware manipulation can be learned offline and deployed without direct haptic feedback.

Abstract: Tactile sensing is a crucial capability for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) architectures, as it enables dexterous and safe manipulation in contact-rich tasks. However, reliance on dedicated tactile hardware increases cost and reduces reproducibility across robotic platforms. We argue that tactile-aware manipulation can be learned offline and deployed without direct haptic feedback at inference. To this end, we present HapticVLA, which proceeds in two tightly coupled stages: Safety-Aware Reward-Weighted Flow Matching (SA-RWFM) and Tactile Distillation (TD). SA-RWFM trains a flow-matching action expert that incorporates precomputed, safety-aware tactile rewards penalizing excessive grasping force and suboptimal grasping trajectories. TD further transfers this tactile-aware capability into a conventional VLA: we distill a compact tactile token from the SA-RWFM teacher and train a student VLA to predict that token from vision and state modalities, enabling tactile-aware action generation at inference without requiring on-board tactile sensors. This design preserves contact-rich tactile-aware reasoning within VLA while removing the need for on-board tactile sensors during deployment. On real-world experiments, HapticVLA achieves a mean success rate of 86.7%, consistently outperforming baseline VLAs - including versions provided with direct tactile feedback during inference.

</details>


### [207] [MoE-ACT: Scaling Multi-Task Bimanual Manipulation with Sparse Language-Conditioned Mixture-of-Experts Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15265)
*Kangjun Guo,Haichao Liu,Yanji Sun,Ruhan Zhao,Jinni Zhou,Jun Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MoE-ACT enhances multi-task bimanual manipulation by integrating sparse Mixture-of-Experts into ACT's Transformer encoder, using adaptive expert activation and FiLM modulation to reduce task interference and improve generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current general robotic policies suffer from task interference and negative transfer when handling out-of-distribution variations across multiple tasks, limiting deployment in real-world household and industrial applications.

Method: Integrates sparse Mixture-of-Experts modules into ACT's Transformer encoder to decompose unified policy into independent expert components, uses FiLM for dynamic action token modulation, multi-scale cross-attention for visual features, and incorporates textual conditioning for vision-language action generation.

Result: Outperforms vanilla ACT by average 33% success rate in multi-task performance, demonstrates stronger robustness and generalization in both simulation and real-world dual-arm setups.

Conclusion: MoE-ACT provides an effective lightweight framework for multi-task bimanual manipulation that reduces task interference through expert decomposition and adaptive modulation, enabling better generalization in complex environments.

Abstract: The ability of robots to handle multiple tasks under a unified policy is critical for deploying embodied intelligence in real-world household and industrial applications. However, out-of-distribution variation across tasks often causes severe task interference and negative transfer when training general robotic policies. To address this challenge, we propose a lightweight multi-task imitation learning framework for bimanual manipulation, termed Mixture-of-Experts-Enhanced Action Chunking Transformer (MoE-ACT), which integrates sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) modules into the Transformer encoder of ACT. The MoE layer decomposes a unified task policy into independently invoked expert components. Through adaptive activation, it naturally decouples multi-task action distributions in latent space. During decoding, Feature-wise Linear Modulation (FiLM) dynamically modulates action tokens to improve consistency between action generation and task instructions. In parallel, multi-scale cross-attention enables the policy to simultaneously focus on both low-level and high-level semantic features, providing rich visual information for robotic manipulation. We further incorporate textual information, transitioning the framework from a purely vision-based model to a vision-centric, language-conditioned action generation system. Experimental validation in both simulation and a real-world dual-arm setup shows that MoE-ACT substantially improves multi-task performance. Specifically, MoE-ACT outperforms vanilla ACT by an average of 33% in success rate. These results indicate that MoE-ACT provides stronger robustness and generalization in complex multi-task bimanual manipulation environments. Our open-source project page can be found at https://j3k7.github.io/MoE-ACT/.

</details>


### [208] [GNIO: Gated Neural Inertial Odometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15281)
*Dapeng Feng,Yizhen Yin,Zhiqiang Chen,Yuhua Qi,Hongbo Chen*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GNIO introduces a learning-based inertial odometry framework with Motion Bank and Gated Prediction Head to reduce drift in low-cost MEMS navigation by modeling motion validity and context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-cost MEMS sensors suffer from rapid drift due to noise and bias instability. Existing data-driven approaches struggle with micro-drifts during stationarity and mode fusion during complex motion transitions due to fixed-window regression limitations.

Method: GNIO features two key innovations: 1) a learnable Motion Bank that queries a global dictionary of motion patterns for semantic context beyond local receptive field, and 2) a Gated Prediction Head that decomposes displacement into magnitude and direction, acting as a soft, differentiable Zero-Velocity Update (ZUPT) to dynamically suppress noise during stationary periods while scaling predictions during dynamic motion.

Result: Extensive experiments across four public benchmarks show GNIO significantly reduces position drift compared to state-of-the-art CNN and Transformer baselines. Achieves 60.21% reduction in trajectory error on OxIOD dataset and exhibits superior generalization in challenging scenarios with frequent stops and irregular motion speeds.

Conclusion: GNIO effectively addresses drift issues in low-cost MEMS inertial navigation by explicitly modeling motion validity and context through its Motion Bank and Gated Prediction Head architecture, demonstrating substantial improvements over existing approaches.

Abstract: Inertial navigation using low-cost MEMS sensors is plagued by rapid drift due to sensor noise and bias instability. While recent data-driven approaches have made significant strides, they often struggle with micro-drifts during stationarity and mode fusion during complex motion transitions due to their reliance on fixed-window regression. In this work, we introduce Gated Neural Inertial Odometry (GNIO), a novel learning-based framework that explicitly models motion validity and context. We propose two key architectural innovations: \ding{182} a learnable Motion Bank that queries a global dictionary of motion patterns to provide semantic context beyond the local receptive field, and \ding{183} a Gated Prediction Head that decomposes displacement into magnitude and direction. This gating mechanism acts as a soft, differentiable Zero-Velocity Update (ZUPT), dynamically suppressing sensor noise during stationary periods while scaling predictions during dynamic motion. Extensive experiments across four public benchmarks demonstrate that GNIO significantly reduces position drift compared to state-of-the-art CNN and Transformer-based baselines. Notably, GNIO achieves a $60.21\%$ reduction in trajectory error on the OxIOD dataset and exhibits superior generalization in challenging scenarios involving frequent stops and irregular motion speeds.

</details>


### [209] [User-Tailored Learning to Forecast Walking Modes for Exosuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15329)
*Gabriele Abbate,Enrica Tricomi,Nathalie Gierden,Alessandro Giusti,Lorenzo Masia,Antonio Paolillo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ML-based perception module for exosuits estimates 3 walking modes using only 2 inertial sensors, supports future/past predictions, enables self-labeling for online adaptation, and integrates with closed-loop control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Exosuits need advanced perception systems to adapt to diverse users, but have limited sensory equipment due to weight and wearability constraints. Current systems lack sufficient sensing capabilities for robust walking mode estimation.

Method: Developed a machine learning perception module using only inertial data from two sensors to estimate three walking modes (ascending/descending stairs, level ground). The approach provides estimates for both future and past timesteps, enabling self-labeling for online model adaptation and user-tailored refinement.

Result: Thorough analysis on real-life datasets demonstrates effectiveness of the user-tailored perception module. Successful integration with exosuit in closed-loop controller validated through online single-subject experiment.

Conclusion: The proposed perception system enables lightweight, adaptive exosuit control using minimal sensor data, addressing the trade-off between sensing capability and wearability constraints in assistive robotic devices.

Abstract: Assistive robotic devices, like soft lower-limb exoskeletons or exosuits, are widely spreading with the promise of helping people in everyday life. To make such systems adaptive to the variety of users wearing them, it is desirable to endow exosuits with advanced perception systems. However, exosuits have little sensory equipment because they need to be light and easy to wear. This paper presents a perception module based on machine learning that aims at estimating 3 walking modes (i.e., ascending or descending stairs and walking on level ground) of users wearing an exosuit. We tackle this perception problem using only inertial data from two sensors. Our approach provides an estimate for both future and past timesteps that supports control and enables a self-labeling procedure for online model adaptation. Indeed, we show that our estimate can label data acquired online and refine the model for new users. A thorough analysis carried out on real-life datasets shows the effectiveness of our user-tailored perception module. Finally, we integrate our system with the exosuit in a closed-loop controller, validating its performance in an online single-subject experiment.

</details>


### [210] [NavThinker: Action-Conditioned World Models for Coupled Prediction and Planning in Social Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15359)
*Tianshuai Hu,Zeying Gong,Lingdong Kong,XiaoDong Mei,Yiyi Ding,Qi Zeng,Ao Liang,Rong Li,Yangyi Zhong,Junwei Liang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: NavThinker is a future-aware social navigation framework that combines an action-conditioned world model with on-policy RL to enable robots to reason about future scene evolution and human motion for safer navigation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Social navigation requires robots to anticipate future scene dynamics rather than just react to current observations, creating a coupled prediction-planning challenge where robot actions and human motion mutually influence each other.

Method: NavThinker couples an action-conditioned world model operating in Depth Anything V2 patch feature space with DD-PPO reinforcement learning. The world model performs autoregressive prediction of future scene geometry and human motion, with multi-head decoders producing future depth maps and trajectories. The policy is trained with world-model think-ahead signals via action-conditioned future features fused into current observations and social reward shaping from predicted human trajectories.

Result: State-of-the-art navigation success on single- and multi-robot Social-HM3D benchmarks, with zero-shot transfer to Social-MP3D and successful real-world deployment on a Unitree Go2 robot, demonstrating strong generalization and practical applicability.

Conclusion: NavThinker effectively addresses the coupled prediction-planning challenge in social navigation by enabling future-aware reasoning through world model integration, achieving superior performance and demonstrating real-world viability.

Abstract: Social navigation requires robots to act safely in dynamic human environments. Effective behavior demands thinking ahead: reasoning about how the scene and pedestrians evolve under different robot actions rather than reacting to current observations alone. This creates a coupled prediction-planning challenge, where robot actions and human motion mutually influence each other. To address this challenge, we propose NavThinker, a future-aware framework that couples an action-conditioned world model with on-policy reinforcement learning. The world model operates in the Depth Anything V2 patch feature space and performs autoregressive prediction of future scene geometry and human motion; multi-head decoders then produce future depth maps and human trajectories, yielding a future-aware state aligned with traversability and interaction risk. Crucially, we train the policy with DD-PPO while injecting world-model think-ahead signals via: (i) action-conditioned future features fused into the current observation embedding and (ii) social reward shaping from predicted human trajectories. Experiments on single- and multi-robot Social-HM3D show state-of-the-art navigation success, with zero-shot transfer to Social-MP3D and real-world deployment on a Unitree Go2, validating generalization and practical applicability. Webpage: https://github.com/hutslib/NavThinker.

</details>


### [211] [End-to-End Dexterous Grasp Learning from Single-View Point Clouds via a Multi-Object Scene Dataset](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.15410)
*Tao Geng,Dapeng Yang,Ziwei Liu,Le Zhang,Le Qi,WangYang Li,Yi Ren,Shan Luo,Fenglei Ni*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DGS-Net: An end-to-end grasp prediction network for dexterous grasping in multi-object scenes, trained on a novel dataset with 307 objects, 240 scenes, and 350k+ validated grasps, achieving 88.63% simulation and 78.98% real-world success rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current grasping datasets focus on single-object scenarios with predefined grasp configurations, neglecting environmental interference and dexterous pre-grasp gesture modeling, limiting real-world generalizability.

Method: Propose DGS-Net, an end-to-end network learning dense grasp configurations from single-view point clouds in multi-object scenes.
